I’m there, I’m helping, I’m chopping or stirring or cleaning up afterward, and on the outside I probably look normal enough.\n\nBut inside it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I know those moments are supposed to feel warm or fun or comforting, and I want them to, but mostly I’m just going through the motions. The enjoyment feels muted, like it’s happening somewhere else and I’m not fully connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it very closely. I mean, I sleep, but I don’t know if I’d call it good sleep or bad sleep without thinking about it more. Some nights feel normal enough, and other times I’m just aware that I don’t feel especially rested, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the sleep itself or just… how I’ve been feeling overall.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything really clear with my appetite. I mean, I eat, and meals still happen, especially if I’m with my husband or family and we’re cooking or sitting down together. I’m not sure I’m paying enough attention to say whether it’s changed in a definite way.\n\nSome days food just feels kind of… practical, I guess. Like something I’m supposed to do because it’s mealtime, not because I’m especially excited about it. But I can’t honestly say I’ve had a big increase or decrease, or anything dramatic like that. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to give you a firm answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I’ve done that I feel guilty about, like some event I keep replaying or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes wonder if I’m not reacting the way I’m supposed to with people, if that makes sense. Like if my husband is trying to be kind, or family is trying to include me, and I’m just not really… fully there. Then afterward I might think, “Was I cold? Did I seem ungrateful?” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly. It’s more like second-guessing myself, or feeling bad that I can’t quite meet the moment the way I think I should.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. Not guilty about anything concrete. More just this vague feeling that maybe I’m letting people down somehow, even if I can’t point to a real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve had anything specific like a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s not something I’ve been sitting there mapping out or anything like that.\n\nSometimes when things feel really heavy, I might have thoughts that are more like… I don’t know, like wishing I could just not have to deal with everything for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. I’m not sure I’m paying enough attention to say whether it’s changed in a definite way.\n\nSome days food just feels kind of… practical, I guess. Like something I’m supposed to do because it’s mealtime, not because I’m especially excited about it. But I can’t honestly say I’ve had a big increase or decrease, or anything dramatic like that. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to give you a firm answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I’ve done that I feel guilty about, like some event I keep replaying or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes wonder if I’m not reacting the way I’m supposed to with people, if that makes sense. Like if my husband is trying to be kind, or family is trying to include me, and I’m just not really… fully there. Then afterward I might think, “Was I cold? Did I seem ungrateful?” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly. It’s more like second-guessing myself, or feeling bad that I can’t quite meet the moment the way I think I should.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. Not guilty about anything concrete. More just this vague feeling that maybe I’m letting people down somehow, even if I can’t point to a real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve had anything specific like a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s not something I’ve been sitting there mapping out or anything like that.\n\nSometimes when things feel really heavy, I might have thoughts that are more like… I don’t know, like wishing I could just not have to deal with everything for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. Nothing is wrong in the room, exactly. People are talking, there’s food, someone says something funny, and I know I should feel warmed by it or kind of… lifted. And sometimes I can smile or laugh because that’s what you do, and part of me does know I’m lucky to have those people around me.\n\nBut most days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. I’m present, I’m going through the motions, I’m helping chop vegetables or setting the table or listening to my husband tell me about his day, but the pleasure doesn’t really land. It’s like I can see that something should feel nice, but I can’t quite feel it fully.\n\nI still do those things because I don’t want to withdraw completely, and because I care about my family. But it’s not the same as before, when I could actually relax into it and enjoy it without having to think so much about acting normal.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the biggest thing I notice, actually. It’s not that I stopped doing everything, because I haven’t. I still cook with my husband sometimes, or we’ll have family over, and I’ll help get things ready and sit with everyone and talk. Those used to be things I really looked forward to — not in some dramatic way, just normal comfort, you know? Being in the kitchen together, laughing about something small, feeling like the house was warm and alive.\n\nNow I still do them, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t really come through. I can go through all the motions, and I know in my head that it should be pleasant, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. My husband can be kind, everyone can be perfectly nice, and I’ll be there physically, but I don’t feel the enjoyment the way I used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll smile or laugh, and it’s not fake exactly, but it feels… thinner, I guess. Like I’m responding because I know that’s what the moment calls for, not because I’m really feeling it deeply. So yes, things I used to enjoy are still there, but the pleasure in them feels muted or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and I don’t want to say “nothing” because that sounds so absolute… but honestly, the pleasure is very hard to find most days.\n\nThere are still things I participate in. Like I said, my husband and I might cook together, or the family might be around, and I’ll be there. I’ll chop things, set the table, make conversation, laugh if someone says something funny. And those are the kinds of things that used to make me feel settled and happy in a quiet way.\n\nBut now it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband is being kind, even when nothing is actually wrong, I feel like I’m going through the motions more than enjoying it. I can recognize that the moment should feel good, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nSo… I still do some of the same activities, but whether I really find pleasure in them? Not much. It’s muted, I guess. Like I’m present, but the enjoyment is somewhere just out of reach.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this current episode of depression?\n\nPatient: It’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time I was moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, where I was mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just stress from the transition, because it was a lot more responsibility and there were all these new expectations. I kept telling myself I’d adjust once things settled down.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way things felt, month after month. So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.

At the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.

But that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. It’s not like there was one dramatic incident or one awful day that I can point to. It was more that the added responsibility and pressure seemed to mark the beginning of things feeling heavier, and then it just… didn’t really go away.

The enjoyment feels muted, like it’s happening somewhere else and I’m not fully connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it very closely. I mean, I sleep, but I don’t know if I’d call it good sleep or bad sleep without thinking about it more. Some nights feel normal enough, and other times I’m just aware that I don’t feel especially rested, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the sleep itself or just… how I’ve been feeling overall.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t want to say yes or no too confidently. Did I seem ungrateful?” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly. It’s more like second-guessing myself, or feeling bad that I can’t quite meet the moment the way I think I should.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. Not guilty about anything concrete. More just this vague feeling that maybe I’m letting people down somehow, even if I can’t point to a real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve had anything specific like a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s not something I’ve been sitting there mapping out or anything like that.\n\nSometimes when things feel really heavy, I might have thoughts that are more like… I don’t know, like wishing I could just not have to deal with everything for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. Nothing is wrong in the room, exactly. People are talking, there’s food, someone says something funny, and I know I should feel warmed by it or kind of… lifted. And sometimes I can smile or laugh because that’s what you do, and part of me does know I’m lucky to have those people around me.\n\nBut most days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. I’m present, I’m going through the motions, I’m helping chop vegetables or setting the table or listening to my husband tell me about his day, but the pleasure doesn’t really land. It’s like I can see that something should feel nice, but I can’t quite feel it fully.\n\nI still do those things because I don’t want to withdraw completely, and because I care about my family. But it’s not the same as before, when I could actually relax into it and enjoy it without having to think so much about acting normal.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the biggest thing I notice, actually. It’s not that I stopped doing everything, because I haven’t. I still cook with my husband sometimes, or we’ll have family over, and I’ll help get things ready and sit with everyone and talk. Those used to be things I really looked forward to — not in some dramatic way, just normal comfort, you know? Being in the kitchen together, laughing about something small, feeling like the house was warm and alive.\n\nNow I still do them, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t really come through. I can go through all the motions, and I know in my head that it should be pleasant, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. My husband can be kind, everyone can be perfectly nice, and I’ll be there physically, but I don’t feel the enjoyment the way I used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll smile or laugh, and it’s not fake exactly, but it feels… thinner, I guess. Like I’m responding because I know that’s what the moment calls for, not because I’m really feeling it deeply. So yes, things I used to enjoy are still there, but the pleasure in them feels muted or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and I don’t want to say “nothing” because that sounds so absolute… but honestly, the pleasure is very hard to find most days.\n\nThere are still things I participate in. Like I said, my husband and I might cook together, or the family might be around, and I’ll be there. I’ll chop things, set the table, make conversation, laugh if someone says something funny. And those are the kinds of things that used to make me feel settled and happy in a quiet way.\n\nBut now it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband is being kind, even when nothing is actually wrong, I feel like I’m going through the motions more than enjoying it. I can recognize that the moment should feel good, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nSo… I still do some of the same activities, but whether I really find pleasure in them? Not much. It’s muted, I guess. Like I’m present, but the enjoyment is somewhere just out of reach.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this current episode of depression?\n\nPatient: It’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time I was moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, where I was mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just stress from the transition, because it was a lot more responsibility and there were all these new expectations. I kept telling myself I’d adjust once things settled down.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way things felt, month after month. So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened around the time you started feeling this way, about three years and three months ago, that you think might have triggered these feelings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.\n\nAt the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.\n\nBut that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. It’s not like there was one dramatic incident or one awful day that I can point to. It was more that the added responsibility and pressure seemed to mark the beginning of things feeling heavier, and then it just… didn’t really go away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened around the time you started feeling this way, about three years and three months ago, that you think might have triggered these feelings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.\n\nAt the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.\n\nBut that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. It’s not like there was one dramatic incident or one awful day that I can point to. It was more that the added responsibility and pressure seemed to mark the beginning of things feeling heavier, and then it just… didn’t really go away.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any periods where your depressive feelings lifted for a while before returning?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, there have been better days here and there, where I’m able to get through things a little more easily or I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel quite as heavy. But I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted and I felt like myself again, and then it came back.

Since that work change — when I was moved into the higher-responsibility role mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems — it’s been pretty continuous. That was about three years and three months ago now, and it’s felt like it just settled in and stayed there. Some days are less awful than others, but I don’t remember any meaningful break from it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… tired all the time. Not just “I had a busy day” tired, but like my body and my brain are already running on empty way too early. I work as a claims adjuster, and by midmorning—after I’ve only been reviewing files or walking a junior adjuster through a claim—I feel like I’ve used up everything I had for the day. It’s frustrating because it doesn’t seem like I’ve done enough to be that wiped out, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but most days there’s this sort of gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. Like things are happening, and I’m there, and I can respond the way I’m supposed to, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nEven when my husband is being really kind, or we’re cooking with the family and everyone’s talking, I can go through all the motions—chop the vegetables, laugh at the right moment, help clean up—and part of me knows, “This should feel nice.” But it just feels muted. Like I’m separated from it by a layer of glass or something. I wouldn’t say I’m sad every single second, but most days, yes, I feel down in that heavy, dulled-out kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things that you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy things the way I used to. I still do them, but it’s more like I’m acting out the steps of enjoying them, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with my husband—he’ll be sweet or thoughtful, and I can recognize that he’s being kind, and I’ll respond, I’ll smile or say thank you, but it doesn’t land inside me the way it should. Or when we’re cooking with the family, which used to be something I really liked… everyone talking over each other, passing things around, joking, all of that. I’m there, I’m helping, I’m chopping or stirring or cleaning up afterward, and on the outside I probably look normal enough.\n\nBut inside it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. I know those moments are supposed to feel warm or fun or comforting, and I want them to, but mostly I’m just going through the motions. The enjoyment feels muted, like it’s happening somewhere else and I’m not fully connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it very closely. I mean, I sleep, but I don’t know if I’d call it good sleep or bad sleep without thinking about it more. Some nights feel normal enough, and other times I’m just aware that I don’t feel especially rested, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the sleep itself or just… how I’ve been feeling overall.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything really clear with my appetite. I mean, I eat, and meals still happen, especially if I’m with my husband or family and we’re cooking or sitting down together. I’m not sure I’m paying enough attention to say whether it’s changed in a definite way.\n\nSome days food just feels kind of… practical, I guess. Like something I’m supposed to do because it’s mealtime, not because I’m especially excited about it. But I can’t honestly say I’ve had a big increase or decrease, or anything dramatic like that. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to give you a firm answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I’ve done that I feel guilty about, like some event I keep replaying or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes wonder if I’m not reacting the way I’m supposed to with people, if that makes sense. Like if my husband is trying to be kind, or family is trying to include me, and I’m just not really… fully there. Then afterward I might think, “Was I cold? Did I seem ungrateful?” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly. It’s more like second-guessing myself, or feeling bad that I can’t quite meet the moment the way I think I should.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. Not guilty about anything concrete. More just this vague feeling that maybe I’m letting people down somehow, even if I can’t point to a real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve had anything specific like a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s not something I’ve been sitting there mapping out or anything like that.\n\nSometimes when things feel really heavy, I might have thoughts that are more like… I don’t know, like wishing I could just not have to deal with everything for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. Nothing is wrong in the room, exactly. People are talking, there’s food, someone says something funny, and I know I should feel warmed by it or kind of… lifted. And sometimes I can smile or laugh because that’s what you do, and part of me does know I’m lucky to have those people around me.\n\nBut most days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. I’m present, I’m going through the motions, I’m helping chop vegetables or setting the table or listening to my husband tell me about his day, but the pleasure doesn’t really land. It’s like I can see that something should feel nice, but I can’t quite feel it fully.\n\nI still do those things because I don’t want to withdraw completely, and because I care about my family. But it’s not the same as before, when I could actually relax into it and enjoy it without having to think so much about acting normal.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the biggest thing I notice, actually. It’s not that I stopped doing everything, because I haven’t. I still cook with my husband sometimes, or we’ll have family over, and I’ll help get things ready and sit with everyone and talk. Those used to be things I really looked forward to — not in some dramatic way, just normal comfort, you know? Being in the kitchen together, laughing about something small, feeling like the house was warm and alive.\n\nNow I still do them, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t really come through. I can go through all the motions, and I know in my head that it should be pleasant, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. My husband can be kind, everyone can be perfectly nice, and I’ll be there physically, but I don’t feel the enjoyment the way I used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll smile or laugh, and it’s not fake exactly, but it feels… thinner, I guess. Like I’m responding because I know that’s what the moment calls for, not because I’m really feeling it deeply. So yes, things I used to enjoy are still there, but the pleasure in them feels muted or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and I don’t want to say “nothing” because that sounds so absolute… but honestly, the pleasure is very hard to find most days.\n\nThere are still things I participate in. Like I said, my husband and I might cook together, or the family might be around, and I’ll be there. I’ll chop things, set the table, make conversation, laugh if someone says something funny. And those are the kinds of things that used to make me feel settled and happy in a quiet way.\n\nBut now it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband is being kind, even when nothing is actually wrong, I feel like I’m going through the motions more than enjoying it. I can recognize that the moment should feel good, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nSo… I still do some of the same activities, but whether I really find pleasure in them? Not much. It’s muted, I guess. Like I’m present, but the enjoyment is somewhere just out of reach.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this current episode of depression?\n\nPatient: It’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time I was moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, where I was mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just stress from the transition, because it was a lot more responsibility and there were all these new expectations. I kept telling myself I’d adjust once things settled down.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way things felt, month after month. So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened around the time you started feeling this way, about three years and three months ago, that you think might have triggered these feelings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.\n\nAt the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.\n\nBut that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. But I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted and I felt like myself again, and then it came back.\n\nSince that work change — when I was moved into the higher-responsibility role mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems — it’s been pretty continuous. Did I seem ungrateful?” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly. It’s more like second-guessing myself, or feeling bad that I can’t quite meet the moment the way I think I should.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. Not guilty about anything concrete. More just this vague feeling that maybe I’m letting people down somehow, even if I can’t point to a real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve had anything specific like a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s not something I’ve been sitting there mapping out or anything like that.\n\nSometimes when things feel really heavy, I might have thoughts that are more like… I don’t know, like wishing I could just not have to deal with everything for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. Nothing is wrong in the room, exactly. People are talking, there’s food, someone says something funny, and I know I should feel warmed by it or kind of… lifted. And sometimes I can smile or laugh because that’s what you do, and part of me does know I’m lucky to have those people around me.\n\nBut most days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. I’m present, I’m going through the motions, I’m helping chop vegetables or setting the table or listening to my husband tell me about his day, but the pleasure doesn’t really land. It’s like I can see that something should feel nice, but I can’t quite feel it fully.\n\nI still do those things because I don’t want to withdraw completely, and because I care about my family. But it’s not the same as before, when I could actually relax into it and enjoy it without having to think so much about acting normal.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the biggest thing I notice, actually. It’s not that I stopped doing everything, because I haven’t. I still cook with my husband sometimes, or we’ll have family over, and I’ll help get things ready and sit with everyone and talk. Those used to be things I really looked forward to — not in some dramatic way, just normal comfort, you know? Being in the kitchen together, laughing about something small, feeling like the house was warm and alive.\n\nNow I still do them, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t really come through. I can go through all the motions, and I know in my head that it should be pleasant, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. My husband can be kind, everyone can be perfectly nice, and I’ll be there physically, but I don’t feel the enjoyment the way I used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll smile or laugh, and it’s not fake exactly, but it feels… thinner, I guess. Like I’m responding because I know that’s what the moment calls for, not because I’m really feeling it deeply. So yes, things I used to enjoy are still there, but the pleasure in them feels muted or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and I don’t want to say “nothing” because that sounds so absolute… but honestly, the pleasure is very hard to find most days.\n\nThere are still things I participate in. Like I said, my husband and I might cook together, or the family might be around, and I’ll be there. I’ll chop things, set the table, make conversation, laugh if someone says something funny. And those are the kinds of things that used to make me feel settled and happy in a quiet way.\n\nBut now it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband is being kind, even when nothing is actually wrong, I feel like I’m going through the motions more than enjoying it. I can recognize that the moment should feel good, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nSo… I still do some of the same activities, but whether I really find pleasure in them? Not much. It’s muted, I guess. Like I’m present, but the enjoyment is somewhere just out of reach.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this current episode of depression?\n\nPatient: It’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time I was moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, where I was mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just stress from the transition, because it was a lot more responsibility and there were all these new expectations. I kept telling myself I’d adjust once things settled down.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way things felt, month after month. So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened around the time you started feeling this way, about three years and three months ago, that you think might have triggered these feelings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.\n\nAt the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.\n\nBut that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. It’s not like there was one dramatic incident or one awful day that I can point to. It was more that the added responsibility and pressure seemed to mark the beginning of things feeling heavier, and then it just… didn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your depressive feelings lifted for a while before returning?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there have been better days here and there, where I’m able to get through things a little more easily or I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel quite as heavy. But I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted and I felt like myself again, and then it came back.\n\nSince that work change — when I was moved into the higher-responsibility role mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems — it’s been pretty continuous. That was about three years and three months ago now, and it’s felt like it just settled in and stayed there. Some days are less awful than others, but I don’t remember any meaningful break from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But I don’t know if that counts as “hurting myself.” I haven’t really talked about it in those terms.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear thing to tell you, like “yes, I want to do this.” But I also don’t want to pretend I never have those darker thoughts where I just feel worn down and tired of being in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I’ve had thoughts in that clear of a way, like “I want to end my life” as a decision or something I’m planning. When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life. I don’t have some specific plan or anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more like wanting relief, I suppose, than wanting to do something definite.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. It’s hard to separate out sometimes, because the thoughts can be blurry, and then when I try to explain them out loud they sound more serious than they feel in the moment. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having clear thoughts about ending my life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. About three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI think it really started around when I got moved into that higher-responsibility role at work — mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, you know, like anyone would feel stretched taking on more responsibility. So I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it.\n\nBut it didn’t really feel like it ever lifted in a lasting way. It’s kind of been there since then, in the background and sometimes more in the foreground. So… yes, about three years and a few months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m just tired all the time in a way that doesn’t feel like normal tired.\n\nI notice it most at work. Like, by midmorning, after I’ve only reviewed some files or walked one of the junior adjusters through a claim, I already feel like I’m running on empty. And it’s not even that I’ve done something physically demanding. I can just be sitting there going through documentation, trying to pay attention to details, and it feels like my body is heavy and my brain is sort of… dragging behind me.\n\nI used to be able to get through that kind of work without thinking about it so much. Now I feel like I’m constantly pushing myself through the day, even early in the day, like I’m starting with only half a tank. It’s frustrating because from the outside I probably look like I’m doing the same things, but internally it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, not in a way I’ve really noticed or that anyone has commented on.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number or anything. Sometimes my clothes feel a little different depending on the week, but that’s always been kind of normal for me, and I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop or gain recently. If there has been a change, it hasn’t been significant enough for me to really register it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling down or depressed like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I’ve had sad periods before, like most people do, or times when I was stressed or discouraged, but I don’t know that I’d call them the same as this.\n\nI’m trying to think back over my life and not overstate it. There were times when I felt low, sure, but I don’t remember it being this kind of long, steady thing where it just sort of follows me around for years. So… I guess my answer is I’m not aware of having had something like this before, at least not in a way that stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: There are things I still do, yes, but I don’t know that I’d say I really enjoy them the way I used to.\n\nLike my husband is very kind, and we’ll have evenings where we’re cooking together or the family is over, and on paper those are good moments. Nothing is wrong in the room, exactly. People are talking, there’s food, someone says something funny, and I know I should feel warmed by it or kind of… lifted. And sometimes I can smile or laugh because that’s what you do, and part of me does know I’m lucky to have those people around me.\n\nBut most days it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That’s the best way I can describe it. I’m present, I’m going through the motions, I’m helping chop vegetables or setting the table or listening to my husband tell me about his day, but the pleasure doesn’t really land. It’s like I can see that something should feel nice, but I can’t quite feel it fully.\n\nI still do those things because I don’t want to withdraw completely, and because I care about my family. But it’s not the same as before, when I could actually relax into it and enjoy it without having to think so much about acting normal.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the biggest thing I notice, actually. It’s not that I stopped doing everything, because I haven’t. I still cook with my husband sometimes, or we’ll have family over, and I’ll help get things ready and sit with everyone and talk. Those used to be things I really looked forward to — not in some dramatic way, just normal comfort, you know? Being in the kitchen together, laughing about something small, feeling like the house was warm and alive.\n\nNow I still do them, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t really come through. I can go through all the motions, and I know in my head that it should be pleasant, but most days it’s like there’s this gray film over everything. My husband can be kind, everyone can be perfectly nice, and I’ll be there physically, but I don’t feel the enjoyment the way I used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll smile or laugh, and it’s not fake exactly, but it feels… thinner, I guess. Like I’m responding because I know that’s what the moment calls for, not because I’m really feeling it deeply. So yes, things I used to enjoy are still there, but the pleasure in them feels muted or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something, and I don’t want to say “nothing” because that sounds so absolute… but honestly, the pleasure is very hard to find most days.\n\nThere are still things I participate in. Like I said, my husband and I might cook together, or the family might be around, and I’ll be there. I’ll chop things, set the table, make conversation, laugh if someone says something funny. And those are the kinds of things that used to make me feel settled and happy in a quiet way.\n\nBut now it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. Even when my husband is being kind, even when nothing is actually wrong, I feel like I’m going through the motions more than enjoying it. I can recognize that the moment should feel good, but it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to.\n\nSo… I still do some of the same activities, but whether I really find pleasure in them? Not much. It’s muted, I guess. Like I’m present, but the enjoyment is somewhere just out of reach.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this current episode of depression?\n\nPatient: It’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nI can place it pretty clearly because it started around the time I was moved into that higher-responsibility role at work, where I was mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems. At first I thought it was just stress from the transition, because it was a lot more responsibility and there were all these new expectations. I kept telling myself I’d adjust once things settled down.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just kind of became the way things felt, month after month. So, yes… I’d say this current stretch has been going on since then, about three years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened around the time you started feeling this way, about three years and three months ago, that you think might have triggered these feelings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the clearest thing was the change at work. I was moved into that higher-responsibility role, where I wasn’t just doing my own claims work anymore, but also mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee those new workflow systems.\n\nAt the time, I didn’t think of it as some huge turning point. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course I’m stressed, it’s a bigger role, there’s more to keep track of, people are looking to me more.” I thought once I got used to it, or once the systems were running more smoothly, I’d feel like myself again.\n\nBut that’s when it started, as best as I can remember — around three years and three months ago. It’s not like there was one dramatic incident or one awful day that I can point to. It was more that the added responsibility and pressure seemed to mark the beginning of things feeling heavier, and then it just… didn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your depressive feelings lifted for a while before returning?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there have been better days here and there, where I’m able to get through things a little more easily or I’m distracted enough that it doesn’t feel quite as heavy. But I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted and I felt like myself again, and then it came back.\n\nSince that work change — when I was moved into the higher-responsibility role mentoring other adjusters and helping oversee the new workflow systems — it’s been pretty continuous. That was about three years and three months ago now, and it’s felt like it just settled in and stayed there. Some days are less awful than others, but I don’t remember any meaningful break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression before the one that started about three years and three months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had low periods before in my life, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I could point to something before this and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing or a full episode.\n\nThis one is the one I can actually place and describe, because it’s been so ongoing and tied in my mind to that work change. Before that… I don’t know. I don’t want to say something inaccurate. I may have had times where I felt down or worn out, but nothing stands out to me the way this current stretch does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

When you put it that directly, it feels different than just saying I feel tired or overwhelmed.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I’ve had moments where I wish I could just step out of everything, or not have to keep pushing through the same feelings, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as wanting to end my life.
